    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… not sleeping, barely at all, since that warehouse inspection. I keep thinking I’m tired enough that I’ll finally pass out, but then I’m lying there, wide awake, and these bits of it just keep replaying in my head. Not even always in order—just flashes, sounds, the feeling of being near that blast.\n\nAnd when I do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, like my whole body is convinced I’m back there again, and then I’m just wide awake, heart going, and I can’t settle down afterward. It’s been really hard to function like this, and I guess I finally thought… okay, I need to talk to someone, because it’s not going away on its own.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned flashes of the warehouse inspection replaying in your head, can you tell me more about what you mean by flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s like… I’ll be doing something totally normal, like reviewing a policy at work or going through a loss report, and then out of nowhere I get this really sharp image in my head. Not like I’m choosing to think about it—more like it slams into me.\n\nA lot of the time it’s the worker on the floor. I can see him there, and the soot in the air, like it’s hanging there again. And it’s so specific, that’s what bothers me. It’s not just “oh, I remember the incident.” It’s like one frame of it comes back with this horrible clarity, and my body reacts before I even have time to tell myself I’m safe or that I’m at my desk. My stomach drops, my chest tightens, and for a second it feels like I’m right back near the blast instead of sitting in front of a spreadsheet.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nThere’s this warehouse file at work—the actual file connected to the inspection—and I keep finding reasons not to open it. I’ll tell myself I need more coffee first, or I’ll answer two other emails, or I’ll just “come back to it after lunch,” and then suddenly it’s the end of the day and I still haven’t touched it. Even seeing the subject line makes my stomach drop, like my body reacts before I can talk myself through it.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts… I’ve been letting them go to voicemail. Not every single one, but enough that I know it’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty on top of that stuff. But when the phone rings and I see it’s related to a warehouse or manufacturing site, I just freeze for a second and let it ring out. Then I feel guilty because it’s my job, and it’s not fair to other people, but picking it up feels like I’m inviting the whole thing back into the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilty exactly. I mean… I keep turning that word over because it feels like there should be some big clear answer, but I’m not sure there is.\n\nI do get this heavy feeling when I think about it, like I start wondering if I should have noticed something sooner, or done something differently, or reacted faster. But then another part of me knows I wasn’t there to control everything that happened. I was just there doing an inspection. So it’s not like I’m walking around with one clear thought of “this is my fault” all day.\n\nIt’s more… uncomfortable, I guess. Uneasy. Like my mind keeps trying to go back over it and find the moment where something could have changed, but I don’t know that guilt is the main thing. I’m honestly not sure how to separate guilt from just feeling awful about the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve noticed I’m slower with some things, but I don’t know if that’s concentration exactly or if I’m just sort of on edge and second-guessing myself more.\n\nLike at work, I’ll read the same paragraph in a file a couple of times and realize I didn’t really take it in the first time. But then I think, well, maybe that happens to everyone when they’re stressed or tired, you know? It’s not like I can’t function at all. I’m still getting things done, mostly. It just feels like I have to put more effort into staying with whatever’s in front of me.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat down and thought, “Oh, I can’t concentrate anymore.” It’s more that my attention feels kind of… less reliable than usual. But I don’t know how much of that is because of the inspection versus just me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or surprises since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that makes me feel the most embarrassed, because it’s so… visible, I guess.\n\nLike if a printer jams at work and makes that sudden grinding noise, I jump like something terrible is happening. Not just a little flinch—my whole body reacts before I even know what it is. Same thing if a door slams down the hall, or if someone walks up behind my desk and says my name before I’ve noticed they’re there. I’ll jerk in my chair, my heart starts pounding, and then I’m sitting there trying to act normal even though I feel completely shaken.\n\nAnd it takes me a while to come back down. It’s not like I realize, “Oh, it was just the printer,” and then I’m fine immediately. I can tell myself that, but my body doesn’t seem to believe it right away. It can take several minutes before I feel settled again, and during that time I’m tense and kind of bracing for something else. It feels ridiculous when nothing actually happened, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly looking over your shoulder or feeling like you're on guard even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no. I mean… I understand what you’re asking, and I don’t think I’m walking around every second feeling like someone is about to jump out at me or anything like that.\n\nThere are times when I feel a little more aware of what’s around me than I used to, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being “on guard.” Like if I’m in a room, I might notice where people are or if someone comes in, but I don’t know that I’m actually looking over my shoulder all the time. I haven’t caught myself doing that in a really obvious way.\n\nI guess I feel tense sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s specifically because I think I’m unsafe. It’s more hard to explain than that. So… no, not constantly. Or at least, I haven’t noticed it as a major thing the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. I mean, basically since the warehouse inspection—since the explosion happened there. It wasn’t like I felt completely normal for a few days and then this started later; it feels like it’s been tied to that day pretty directly.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it’ll just settle down on its own because it hasn’t been that long in the grand scheme of things, but… yeah, if I’m being honest, it’s been going on the whole week since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the warehouse inspection or memories related to it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I didn’t realize at first that that’s what I was doing, but I’ve been avoiding anything connected to it at work.\n\nThe big one is the warehouse file. I know I need to look at it, because it’s part of my job, but every time I see it in my queue or an email subject line that’s even related to it, my stomach just drops. It’s this immediate reaction, like I want to close the laptop or click away before I even fully read it. I keep telling myself, “Just open it, just deal with it for five minutes,” but then I find some other task that suddenly feels more urgent.\n\nAnd I’ve been letting calls about industrial accounts go to voicemail, which I know isn’t great. I’m not usually like that. Normally I’m pretty on top of calls, even the annoying ones. But if I see a number or a client name that makes me think it might be connected to warehouses or inspections or anything like that, I just… I let it ring. Then I tell myself I’ll listen to the voicemail later when I’m more ready, except later doesn’t really feel any better.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it sounds so small—just emails and phone calls—but even opening those emails makes me feel physically sick for a second, like my body is reacting before I’ve decided anything. So yes, I guess I’m trying not to think about it by avoiding the things that would make me think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems calm and safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “always” is the right word, no. I mean, I get what you’re asking, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally relaxed all the time, because I’m not. But I also wouldn’t say I’m sitting there every minute expecting something terrible to happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I can feel tense or unsettled, and then I start wondering if that means I’m waiting for something, but I’m not sure that’s exactly it. If I’m at home and everything is quiet, I don’t necessarily think, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nMaybe I’m a little more cautious than I used to be, or I notice things more, but I haven’t really noticed this constant sense that danger is coming even when things are calm. So I guess my answer is… not really, or at least not in a way I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve “lost interest,” exactly. I mean, this week has been strange enough that I haven’t really been doing much of my normal stuff, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it anymore or because I just haven’t felt like my usual routine is normal right now.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether I still want to eventually go back to the things I usually like, I think I do. I don’t feel like I suddenly don’t care about them. It’s more that everything feels kind of interrupted, if that makes sense. I’ve been more focused on just getting through the day and dealing with work things, so I haven’t really tested it much.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear loss of interest. Yes… at least at work, I’ve noticed it. I don’t know if I’d say my relationships are falling apart or anything that dramatic, but I definitely haven’t been myself with coworkers.

There was one moment that really stuck with me. A junior analyst asked me a pretty small question about a spreadsheet — honestly, the kind of thing I normally would just answer or walk them through — and I snapped at them. Like, my tone was way sharper than it needed to be, and I heard myself doing it almost as it was happening, but I still did it. It wasn’t yelling exactly, but it was harsh, and they looked kind of taken aback.

Afterward I felt really embarrassed because that’s not how I usually treat people, especially someone junior who’s just trying to do their job. I kept thinking about it afterward, like, “Why did I react like that?” It felt out of proportion to the situation. So yes, there’s been some strain there, mostly from me being more irritable than normal.

So yes, there’s been some strain there, mostly from me being more irritable than normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself being extremely alert to your surroundings, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / insurance_underwriter / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it “extremely alert,” no. I mean, I’ve definitely been more jumpy in certain moments, or I’ll notice something and feel my attention snap to it, but that feels different from being on high alert all the time.

When you say “even when there’s no apparent reason,” I guess I’m trying to think of examples, like sitting at home or being somewhere ordinary… and I don’t think I’m constantly monitoring everything around me. I’m not watching every person who walks by or checking corners or anything like that. It’s more like I get unsettled for a moment if something catches me off guard, and then I kind of try to talk myself down.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a frequent thing. Maybe occasionally tense or keyed up, but not often extremely alert for no reason.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just… not sleeping, barely at all, since that warehouse inspection. I keep thinking I’m tired enough that I’ll finally pass out, but then I’m lying there, wide awake, and these bits of it just keep replaying in my head. Not even always in order—just flashes, sounds, the feeling of being near that blast.\n\nAnd when I do drift off, it doesn’t last. I’ll wake up sweating, like my whole body is convinced I’m back there again, and then I’m just wide awake, heart going, and I can’t settle down afterward. It’s been really hard to function like this, and I guess I finally thought… okay, I need to talk to someone, because it’s not going away on its own.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned flashes of the warehouse inspection replaying in your head, can you tell me more about what you mean by flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s like… I’ll be doing something totally normal, like reviewing a policy at work or going through a loss report, and then out of nowhere I get this really sharp image in my head. Not like I’m choosing to think about it—more like it slams into me.\n\nA lot of the time it’s the worker on the floor. I can see him there, and the soot in the air, like it’s hanging there again. And it’s so specific, that’s what bothers me. It’s not just “oh, I remember the incident.” It’s like one frame of it comes back with this horrible clarity, and my body reacts before I even have time to tell myself I’m safe or that I’m at my desk. My stomach drops, my chest tightens, and for a second it feels like I’m right back near the blast instead of sitting in front of a spreadsheet.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nThere’s this warehouse file at work—the actual file connected to the inspection—and I keep finding reasons not to open it. I’ll tell myself I need more coffee first, or I’ll answer two other emails, or I’ll just “come back to it after lunch,” and then suddenly it’s the end of the day and I still haven’t touched it. Even seeing the subject line makes my stomach drop, like my body reacts before I can talk myself through it.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts… I’ve been letting them go to voicemail. Not every single one, but enough that I know it’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty on top of that stuff. But when the phone rings and I see it’s related to a warehouse or manufacturing site, I just freeze for a second and let it ring out. Then I feel guilty because it’s my job, and it’s not fair to other people, but picking it up feels like I’m inviting the whole thing back into the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilty exactly. I mean… I keep turning that word over because it feels like there should be some big clear answer, but I’m not sure there is.\n\nI do get this heavy feeling when I think about it, like I start wondering if I should have noticed something sooner, or done something differently, or reacted faster. But then another part of me knows I wasn’t there to control everything that happened. I was just there doing an inspection. So it’s not like I’m walking around with one clear thought of “this is my fault” all day.\n\nIt’s more… uncomfortable, I guess. Uneasy. Like my mind keeps trying to go back over it and find the moment where something could have changed, but I don’t know that guilt is the main thing. I’m honestly not sure how to separate guilt from just feeling awful about the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve noticed I’m slower with some things, but I don’t know if that’s concentration exactly or if I’m just sort of on edge and second-guessing myself more.\n\nLike at work, I’ll read the same paragraph in a file a couple of times and realize I didn’t really take it in the first time. But then I think, well, maybe that happens to everyone when they’re stressed or tired, you know? It’s not like I can’t function at all. I’m still getting things done, mostly. It just feels like I have to put more effort into staying with whatever’s in front of me.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat down and thought, “Oh, I can’t concentrate anymore.” It’s more that my attention feels kind of… less reliable than usual. But I don’t know how much of that is because of the inspection versus just me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or surprises since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that makes me feel the most embarrassed, because it’s so… visible, I guess.\n\nLike if a printer jams at work and makes that sudden grinding noise, I jump like something terrible is happening. Not just a little flinch—my whole body reacts before I even know what it is. Same thing if a door slams down the hall, or if someone walks up behind my desk and says my name before I’ve noticed they’re there. I’ll jerk in my chair, my heart starts pounding, and then I’m sitting there trying to act normal even though I feel completely shaken.\n\nAnd it takes me a while to come back down. It’s not like I realize, “Oh, it was just the printer,” and then I’m fine immediately. I can tell myself that, but my body doesn’t seem to believe it right away. It can take several minutes before I feel settled again, and during that time I’m tense and kind of bracing for something else. It feels ridiculous when nothing actually happened, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly looking over your shoulder or feeling like you're on guard even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no. I mean… I understand what you’re asking, and I don’t think I’m walking around every second feeling like someone is about to jump out at me or anything like that.\n\nThere are times when I feel a little more aware of what’s around me than I used to, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being “on guard.” Like if I’m in a room, I might notice where people are or if someone comes in, but I don’t know that I’m actually looking over my shoulder all the time. I haven’t caught myself doing that in a really obvious way.\n\nI guess I feel tense sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s specifically because I think I’m unsafe. It’s more hard to explain than that. So… no, not constantly. Or at least, I haven’t noticed it as a major thing the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. I mean, basically since the warehouse inspection—since the explosion happened there. It wasn’t like I felt completely normal for a few days and then this started later; it feels like it’s been tied to that day pretty directly.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it’ll just settle down on its own because it hasn’t been that long in the grand scheme of things, but… yeah, if I’m being honest, it’s been going on the whole week since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the warehouse inspection or memories related to it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I didn’t realize at first that that’s what I was doing, but I’ve been avoiding anything connected to it at work.\n\nThe big one is the warehouse file. I know I need to look at it, because it’s part of my job, but every time I see it in my queue or an email subject line that’s even related to it, my stomach just drops. It’s this immediate reaction, like I want to close the laptop or click away before I even fully read it. I keep telling myself, “Just open it, just deal with it for five minutes,” but then I find some other task that suddenly feels more urgent.\n\nAnd I’ve been letting calls about industrial accounts go to voicemail, which I know isn’t great. I’m not usually like that. Normally I’m pretty on top of calls, even the annoying ones. But if I see a number or a client name that makes me think it might be connected to warehouses or inspections or anything like that, I just… I let it ring. Then I tell myself I’ll listen to the voicemail later when I’m more ready, except later doesn’t really feel any better.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it sounds so small—just emails and phone calls—but even opening those emails makes me feel physically sick for a second, like my body is reacting before I’ve decided anything. So yes, I guess I’m trying not to think about it by avoiding the things that would make me think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems calm and safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “always” is the right word, no. I mean, I get what you’re asking, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally relaxed all the time, because I’m not. But I also wouldn’t say I’m sitting there every minute expecting something terrible to happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I can feel tense or unsettled, and then I start wondering if that means I’m waiting for something, but I’m not sure that’s exactly it. If I’m at home and everything is quiet, I don’t necessarily think, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nMaybe I’m a little more cautious than I used to be, or I notice things more, but I haven’t really noticed this constant sense that danger is coming even when things are calm. So I guess my answer is… not really, or at least not in a way I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve “lost interest,” exactly. I mean, this week has been strange enough that I haven’t really been doing much of my normal stuff, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it anymore or because I just haven’t felt like my usual routine is normal right now.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether I still want to eventually go back to the things I usually like, I think I do. I don’t feel like I suddenly don’t care about them. It’s more that everything feels kind of interrupted, if that makes sense. I’ve been more focused on just getting through the day and dealing with work things, so I haven’t really tested it much.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear loss of interest. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having difficulty falling asleep or staying asleep due to being constantly on the lookout for potential threats?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I feel like I’ve barely been sleeping since the warehouse inspection.\n\nWhen I get into bed, I’m exhausted, like my body is completely drained, but the second it gets quiet my mind starts going back to it. I’ll lie there replaying little pieces of what happened, not even always in order. It’s like my brain keeps pulling me back to the warehouse and that moment near the blast, and then I’m wide awake even though I was tired a minute before.\n\nAnd when I do finally drift off, it doesn’t feel like real sleep for very long. I’ll wake up sweaty and startled, like I’m suddenly right back there again, and it takes me a while to understand that I’m in my room and nothing is happening. After that I’m just lying there with my heart going, too awake to fall back asleep.\n\nI don’t know if I’d describe it as calmly “looking out for threats” exactly, but it feels like my body won’t stand down. Like it’s waiting or bracing, even when I know I’m just in bed and I’m safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in situations where you know you are safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not constantly, at least. I’m trying to be careful with how I answer that, because I don’t want to say “no” too quickly and then realize I’m missing something.\n\nI do notice things more than I probably used to, like if there’s a loud noise or someone moves suddenly, I might look over or feel kind of alert for a second. But I wouldn’t say I’m walking around actively scanning every room for exits or checking people or expecting danger everywhere. It’s not like I go into the grocery store and map out what could go wrong, or sit in my apartment watching the door the whole time.\n\nSometimes I feel tense and I catch myself looking around more, but it’s hard to tell whether that’s because I’m searching for danger or just because I’m unsettled in general. So… no, I haven’t really noticed constant scanning, especially not in places where I know I’m safe. It’s more occasional, if anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the warehouse file and related work tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. Basically since the warehouse inspection where the explosion happened.\n\nI didn’t really sit down and decide, “I’m going to avoid that file,” but from right after that day, I just… haven’t been able to make myself deal with it in a normal way. Every time the warehouse file or anything connected to that inspection comes up, I put it off or find some other task to do first. So, yeah, the whole week since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at work, I’ve noticed it. I don’t know if I’d say my relationships are falling apart or anything that dramatic, but I definitely haven’t been myself with coworkers.\n\nThere was one moment that really stuck with me. A junior analyst asked me a pretty small question about a spreadsheet — honestly, the kind of thing I normally would just answer or walk them through — and I snapped at them. Like, my tone was way sharper than it needed to be, and I heard myself doing it almost as it was happening, but I still did it. It wasn’t yelling exactly, but it was harsh, and they looked kind of taken aback.\n\nAfterward I felt really embarrassed because that’s not how I usually treat people, especially someone junior who’s just trying to do their job. I kept thinking about it afterward, like, “Why did I react like that?” It felt out of proportion to the situation. So yes, there’s been some strain there, mostly from me being more irritable than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being extremely alert to your surroundings, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “extremely alert,” no. I mean, I’ve definitely been more jumpy in certain moments, or I’ll notice something and feel my attention snap to it, but that feels different from being on high alert all the time.\n\nWhen you say “even when there’s no apparent reason,” I guess I’m trying to think of examples, like sitting at home or being somewhere ordinary… and I don’t think I’m constantly monitoring everything around me. I’m not watching every person who walks by or checking corners or anything like that. It’s more like I get unsettled for a moment if something catches me off guard, and then I kind of try to talk myself down.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a frequent thing. Maybe occasionally tense or keyed up, but not often extremely alert for no reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t feel like I suddenly don’t care about them. It’s more that everything feels kind of interrupted, if that makes sense. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I feel like I’ve barely been sleeping since the warehouse inspection.\n\nWhen I get into bed, I’m exhausted, like my body is completely drained, but the second it gets quiet my mind starts going back to it. I’ll lie there replaying little pieces of what happened, not even always in order. It’s like my brain keeps pulling me back to the warehouse and that moment near the blast, and then I’m wide awake even though I was tired a minute before.\n\nAnd when I do finally drift off, it doesn’t feel like real sleep for very long. I’ll wake up sweaty and startled, like I’m suddenly right back there again, and it takes me a while to understand that I’m in my room and nothing is happening. After that I’m just lying there with my heart going, too awake to fall back asleep.\n\nI don’t know if I’d describe it as calmly “looking out for threats” exactly, but it feels like my body won’t stand down. Like it’s waiting or bracing, even when I know I’m just in bed and I’m safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in situations where you know you are safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not constantly, at least. I’m trying to be careful with how I answer that, because I don’t want to say “no” too quickly and then realize I’m missing something.\n\nI do notice things more than I probably used to, like if there’s a loud noise or someone moves suddenly, I might look over or feel kind of alert for a second. But I wouldn’t say I’m walking around actively scanning every room for exits or checking people or expecting danger everywhere. I’ve been more focused on just getting through the day and dealing with work things, so I haven’t really tested it much.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear loss of interest. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I feel like I’ve barely been sleeping since the warehouse inspection.\n\nWhen I get into bed, I’m exhausted, like my body is completely drained, but the second it gets quiet my mind starts going back to it. I’ll lie there replaying little pieces of what happened, not even always in order. It’s like my brain keeps pulling me back to the warehouse and that moment near the blast, and then I’m wide awake even though I was tired a minute before.\n\nAnd when I do finally drift off, it doesn’t feel like real sleep for very long. I’ll wake up sweaty and startled, like I’m suddenly right back there again, and it takes me a while to understand that I’m in my room and nothing is happening. After that I’m just lying there with my heart going, too awake to fall back asleep.\n\nI don’t know if I’d describe it as calmly “looking out for threats” exactly, but it feels like my body won’t stand down. Like it’s waiting or bracing, even when I know I’m just in bed and I’m safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in situations where you know you are safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not constantly, at least. I’m trying to be careful with how I answer that, because I don’t want to say “no” too quickly and then realize I’m missing something.\n\nI do notice things more than I probably used to, like if there’s a loud noise or someone moves suddenly, I might look over or feel kind of alert for a second. But I wouldn’t say I’m walking around actively scanning every room for exits or checking people or expecting danger everywhere. It’s not like I go into the grocery store and map out what could go wrong, or sit in my apartment watching the door the whole time.\n\nSometimes I feel tense and I catch myself looking around more, but it’s hard to tell whether that’s because I’m searching for danger or just because I’m unsettled in general. So… no, I haven’t really noticed constant scanning, especially not in places where I know I’m safe. It’s more occasional, if anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the warehouse file and related work tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. Basically since the warehouse inspection where the explosion happened.\n\nI didn’t really sit down and decide, “I’m going to avoid that file,” but from right after that day, I just… haven’t been able to make myself deal with it in a normal way. Every time the warehouse file or anything connected to that inspection comes up, I put it off or find some other task to do first. A junior analyst asked me a pretty small question about a spreadsheet — honestly, the kind of thing I normally would just answer or walk them through — and I snapped at them. Like, my tone was way sharper than it needed to be, and I heard myself doing it almost as it was happening, but I still did it. It wasn’t yelling exactly, but it was harsh, and they looked kind of taken aback.\n\nAfterward I felt really embarrassed because that’s not how I usually treat people, especially someone junior who’s just trying to do their job. I kept thinking about it afterward, like, “Why did I react like that?” It felt out of proportion to the situation. So yes, there’s been some strain there, mostly from me being more irritable than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being extremely alert to your surroundings, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “extremely alert,” no. I mean, I’ve definitely been more jumpy in certain moments, or I’ll notice something and feel my attention snap to it, but that feels different from being on high alert all the time.\n\nWhen you say “even when there’s no apparent reason,” I guess I’m trying to think of examples, like sitting at home or being somewhere ordinary… and I don’t think I’m constantly monitoring everything around me. I’m not watching every person who walks by or checking corners or anything like that. Maybe occasionally tense or keyed up, but not often extremely alert for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in things you normally enjoy doing since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve clearly noticed that in a straightforward way.\n\nLike, if you mean have I stopped caring about everything I usually like, I don’t think so. I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.

I mean, I probably haven’t done as much of my usual stuff this week, but it’s hard for me to separate out whether that’s because I’m avoiding it or not enjoying it, versus just feeling like my routine got thrown off and I’m more preoccupied than usual. I’ll tell myself I need more coffee first, or I’ll answer two other emails, or I’ll just “come back to it after lunch,” and then suddenly it’s the end of the day and I still haven’t touched it. Even seeing the subject line makes my stomach drop, like my body reacts before I can talk myself through it.\n\nAnd calls about industrial accounts… I’ve been letting them go to voicemail. Not every single one, but enough that I know it’s not normal for me. I’m usually pretty on top of that stuff. But when the phone rings and I see it’s related to a warehouse or manufacturing site, I just freeze for a second and let it ring out. Then I feel guilty because it’s my job, and it’s not fair to other people, but picking it up feels like I’m inviting the whole thing back into the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilty exactly. I mean… I keep turning that word over because it feels like there should be some big clear answer, but I’m not sure there is.\n\nI do get this heavy feeling when I think about it, like I start wondering if I should have noticed something sooner, or done something differently, or reacted faster. But then another part of me knows I wasn’t there to control everything that happened. I was just there doing an inspection. So it’s not like I’m walking around with one clear thought of “this is my fault” all day.\n\nIt’s more… uncomfortable, I guess. Uneasy. Like my mind keeps trying to go back over it and find the moment where something could have changed, but I don’t know that guilt is the main thing. I’m honestly not sure how to separate guilt from just feeling awful about the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve noticed I’m slower with some things, but I don’t know if that’s concentration exactly or if I’m just sort of on edge and second-guessing myself more.\n\nLike at work, I’ll read the same paragraph in a file a couple of times and realize I didn’t really take it in the first time. But then I think, well, maybe that happens to everyone when they’re stressed or tired, you know? It’s not like I can’t function at all. I’m still getting things done, mostly. It just feels like I have to put more effort into staying with whatever’s in front of me.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat down and thought, “Oh, I can’t concentrate anymore.” It’s more that my attention feels kind of… less reliable than usual. But I don’t know how much of that is because of the inspection versus just me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or surprises since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that makes me feel the most embarrassed, because it’s so… visible, I guess.\n\nLike if a printer jams at work and makes that sudden grinding noise, I jump like something terrible is happening. Not just a little flinch—my whole body reacts before I even know what it is. Same thing if a door slams down the hall, or if someone walks up behind my desk and says my name before I’ve noticed they’re there. I’ll jerk in my chair, my heart starts pounding, and then I’m sitting there trying to act normal even though I feel completely shaken.\n\nAnd it takes me a while to come back down. It’s not like I realize, “Oh, it was just the printer,” and then I’m fine immediately. I can tell myself that, but my body doesn’t seem to believe it right away. It can take several minutes before I feel settled again, and during that time I’m tense and kind of bracing for something else. It feels ridiculous when nothing actually happened, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly looking over your shoulder or feeling like you're on guard even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly, no. I mean… I understand what you’re asking, and I don’t think I’m walking around every second feeling like someone is about to jump out at me or anything like that.\n\nThere are times when I feel a little more aware of what’s around me than I used to, maybe, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being “on guard.” Like if I’m in a room, I might notice where people are or if someone comes in, but I don’t know that I’m actually looking over my shoulder all the time. I haven’t caught myself doing that in a really obvious way.\n\nI guess I feel tense sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s specifically because I think I’m unsafe. It’s more hard to explain than that. So… no, not constantly. Or at least, I haven’t noticed it as a major thing the way you’re describing it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. I mean, basically since the warehouse inspection—since the explosion happened there. It wasn’t like I felt completely normal for a few days and then this started later; it feels like it’s been tied to that day pretty directly.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it’ll just settle down on its own because it hasn’t been that long in the grand scheme of things, but… yeah, if I’m being honest, it’s been going on the whole week since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the warehouse inspection or memories related to it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. I didn’t realize at first that that’s what I was doing, but I’ve been avoiding anything connected to it at work.\n\nThe big one is the warehouse file. I know I need to look at it, because it’s part of my job, but every time I see it in my queue or an email subject line that’s even related to it, my stomach just drops. It’s this immediate reaction, like I want to close the laptop or click away before I even fully read it. I keep telling myself, “Just open it, just deal with it for five minutes,” but then I find some other task that suddenly feels more urgent.\n\nAnd I’ve been letting calls about industrial accounts go to voicemail, which I know isn’t great. I’m not usually like that. Normally I’m pretty on top of calls, even the annoying ones. But if I see a number or a client name that makes me think it might be connected to warehouses or inspections or anything like that, I just… I let it ring. Then I tell myself I’ll listen to the voicemail later when I’m more ready, except later doesn’t really feel any better.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because it sounds so small—just emails and phone calls—but even opening those emails makes me feel physically sick for a second, like my body is reacting before I’ve decided anything. So yes, I guess I’m trying not to think about it by avoiding the things that would make me think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems calm and safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “always” is the right word, no. I mean, I get what you’re asking, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally relaxed all the time, because I’m not. But I also wouldn’t say I’m sitting there every minute expecting something terrible to happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I can feel tense or unsettled, and then I start wondering if that means I’m waiting for something, but I’m not sure that’s exactly it. If I’m at home and everything is quiet, I don’t necessarily think, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s not that clear in my head.\n\nMaybe I’m a little more cautious than I used to be, or I notice things more, but I haven’t really noticed this constant sense that danger is coming even when things are calm. So I guess my answer is… not really, or at least not in a way I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve “lost interest,” exactly. I mean, this week has been strange enough that I haven’t really been doing much of my normal stuff, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it anymore or because I just haven’t felt like my usual routine is normal right now.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether I still want to eventually go back to the things I usually like, I think I do. I don’t feel like I suddenly don’t care about them. It’s more that everything feels kind of interrupted, if that makes sense. I’ve been more focused on just getting through the day and dealing with work things, so I haven’t really tested it much.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear loss of interest. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having difficulty falling asleep or staying asleep due to being constantly on the lookout for potential threats?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because I feel like I’ve barely been sleeping since the warehouse inspection.\n\nWhen I get into bed, I’m exhausted, like my body is completely drained, but the second it gets quiet my mind starts going back to it. I’ll lie there replaying little pieces of what happened, not even always in order. It’s like my brain keeps pulling me back to the warehouse and that moment near the blast, and then I’m wide awake even though I was tired a minute before.\n\nAnd when I do finally drift off, it doesn’t feel like real sleep for very long. I’ll wake up sweaty and startled, like I’m suddenly right back there again, and it takes me a while to understand that I’m in my room and nothing is happening. After that I’m just lying there with my heart going, too awake to fall back asleep.\n\nI don’t know if I’d describe it as calmly “looking out for threats” exactly, but it feels like my body won’t stand down. Like it’s waiting or bracing, even when I know I’m just in bed and I’m safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in situations where you know you are safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not constantly, at least. I’m trying to be careful with how I answer that, because I don’t want to say “no” too quickly and then realize I’m missing something.\n\nI do notice things more than I probably used to, like if there’s a loud noise or someone moves suddenly, I might look over or feel kind of alert for a second. But I wouldn’t say I’m walking around actively scanning every room for exits or checking people or expecting danger everywhere. It’s not like I go into the grocery store and map out what could go wrong, or sit in my apartment watching the door the whole time.\n\nSometimes I feel tense and I catch myself looking around more, but it’s hard to tell whether that’s because I’m searching for danger or just because I’m unsettled in general. So… no, I haven’t really noticed constant scanning, especially not in places where I know I’m safe. It’s more occasional, if anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the warehouse file and related work tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now. Basically since the warehouse inspection where the explosion happened.\n\nI didn’t really sit down and decide, “I’m going to avoid that file,” but from right after that day, I just… haven’t been able to make myself deal with it in a normal way. Every time the warehouse file or anything connected to that inspection comes up, I put it off or find some other task to do first. So, yeah, the whole week since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at work, I’ve noticed it. I don’t know if I’d say my relationships are falling apart or anything that dramatic, but I definitely haven’t been myself with coworkers.\n\nThere was one moment that really stuck with me. A junior analyst asked me a pretty small question about a spreadsheet — honestly, the kind of thing I normally would just answer or walk them through — and I snapped at them. Like, my tone was way sharper than it needed to be, and I heard myself doing it almost as it was happening, but I still did it. It wasn’t yelling exactly, but it was harsh, and they looked kind of taken aback.\n\nAfterward I felt really embarrassed because that’s not how I usually treat people, especially someone junior who’s just trying to do their job. I kept thinking about it afterward, like, “Why did I react like that?” It felt out of proportion to the situation. So yes, there’s been some strain there, mostly from me being more irritable than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being extremely alert to your surroundings, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “extremely alert,” no. I mean, I’ve definitely been more jumpy in certain moments, or I’ll notice something and feel my attention snap to it, but that feels different from being on high alert all the time.\n\nWhen you say “even when there’s no apparent reason,” I guess I’m trying to think of examples, like sitting at home or being somewhere ordinary… and I don’t think I’m constantly monitoring everything around me. I’m not watching every person who walks by or checking corners or anything like that. It’s more like I get unsettled for a moment if something catches me off guard, and then I kind of try to talk myself down.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a frequent thing. Maybe occasionally tense or keyed up, but not often extremely alert for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in things you normally enjoy doing since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve clearly noticed that in a straightforward way.\n\nLike, if you mean have I stopped caring about everything I usually like, I don’t think so. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.” It’s more that this past week has felt… disrupted, I guess, and I’ve been more focused on getting through the day. So I haven’t been doing as much of my normal stuff, but I’m not sure that’s because I’m less interested in it.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest. Not in a way I could point to confidently. It’s more like things have been off, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as not enjoying things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself participating less in hobbies or activities that you normally find enjoyable since the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed it in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I probably haven’t done as much of my usual stuff this week, but it’s hard for me to separate out whether that’s because I’m avoiding it or not enjoying it, versus just feeling like my routine got thrown off and I’m more preoccupied than usual. Like, if someone asked me, “Have you completely stopped wanting to do things you normally like?” I’d say no, not really. I haven’t had that feeling of, “I don’t care about this anymore.”\n\nIt’s more that I haven’t really been tracking it, if that makes sense. I’ve been sort of going through the week and noticing that things feel off, but I don’t know that I can honestly say I’m participating less because the enjoyment is gone. So… maybe a little less active just because my head hasn’t been in the same place, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a major drop in hobbies or activities I like."
